Today’s Briefing — 2026-11-05

Analysis

Thursday 5 November, the day after the triple-stacked Wednesday, and the tape has turned decisively risk-on and disinflationary. The US midterm result - a Democratic win read by markets as pressure on the Trump administration to wind down the Iran conflict - knocked Brent to ~$96.71/bbl (from ~$105 on 4 Nov and ~$118 a week earlier) and softened the dollar, sending gold to $4,105.70/oz, platinum sharply higher to $1,533, the rand firmer to R16.3568/$ (NEER up to 100.85) and the FTSE/JSE All Share up 3.67% to 113,286 - its strongest day in roughly seven months, per Bloomberg, on a miners' surge; the long end richened in step (R2030 7.85%, R2036 8.59%). This is continuity with the constructive tilt flagged yesterday, only more so: cheaper oil, a stronger rand and a richer curve all pull the same way and materially strengthen the standing hold-into-19-November call rather than disturbing it. On the political leg, early municipal counting (~12% in, per Reuters) has the ANC on ~32% narrowly ahead of the DA on ~30% - too preliminary to read metros from - with the IEC's official results due Sunday 8 November.

Forecast check by 2026-11-21

Judged at deadline 2026-11-21, two business days after the marquee 19 November SARB MPC (the ~25 Nov StatsSA October CPI, 27 Nov Moody's/S&P reviews and 11 Dec Fitch/FOMC fall beyond the window and are flagged watch, not forecast). A directional call AGAINST Nedbank's hike camp, plus the election call now that the IEC count is under way, with commodity and currency bands deliberately tightened from yesterday to reflect today's oil plunge and rand/metals strength (stated changes, not a quiet drift: Brent cap cut from $112 to $108, ZAR upper bound from 17.40 to 17.20, gold floor raised from $3,700 to $3,800 and cap from $4,350 to $4,400, platinum cap raised from $1,675 to $1,725): (a) the SARB MPC on 19 November 2026 HOLDS the repo rate at 7.25% and prime at 10.75% - neither a 25bp hike nor a cut - with no unscheduled inter-meeting move before 21 November (miss if the repo changes at all). (b) Per IEC official or near-final results (official count due Sunday 8 November), the ANC's national aggregate vote share is below 40% AND the DA finishes as the single largest party by vote share in BOTH the City of Johannesburg and the City of Tshwane (miss if either leg fails). (c) ZAR/USD on the SARB EXCX135D feed closes between 16.00 and 17.20 inclusive on every business day of the window (miss if it closes outside on any day). (d) Gold on the gold-api feed closes at or above $3,800/oz AND at or below $4,400/oz on every business day (miss if it breaches either bound on any day). (e) Platinum on the gold-api feed closes below $1,725/oz on every business day (miss if it closes at or above $1,725 on any day). (f) Brent (ICE front-month) closes at or below $108/bbl on every business day - the midterm-driven Iran-de-escalation premium stays deflated (miss if Brent closes above $108 on any business day). Hit if at least five of the six legs hold; partial if three or four hold; miss if two or fewer hold.

Policy desk

Today's moves resolve the inflation question in one direction, so the standing posture firms hard rather than shifts. For the SARB: a ~$97 Brent, a rand back near R16.36 and a richening curve remove whatever imported-fuel case remained for Nedbank's insurance hike, so the MPC should hold at 7.25% on 19 November and keep guidance data-dependent, letting the first post-levy October CPI (~25 Nov) confirm the disinflation before contemplating the cut that the Investec/Old Mutual ~3%-by-April-2027 path implies - a pre-emptive hike into collapsing oil would be a clear policy error. For Treasury: the gold-$4,100 / platinum-$1,533 recovery repairs much of the PGM-and-bullion windfall that underwrote the MTBPS +R17.1bn revenue upgrade, which is the fiscal-credibility dividend to bank into the 27 November Moody's and S&P reviews; keep the fuel-levy relief strictly temporary (through 1 December) rather than yielding to post-election pressure to extend it. On the elections: with the ANC on ~32% early and metros set for coalitions, the binding macro constraint is coalition stability in Johannesburg and Tshwane - that, not the rate path, is what governs service delivery and capex execution from here.

Brent ~$96.71/bbl on 5 Nov, down from ~$105 (4 Nov) and ~$118 a week earlier, as the US midterm Democratic win was read as pressure to end the Iran conflict, deflating the geopolitical premium and softening the dollar (BusinessDay, 5 Nov). Elections: preliminary municipal count with ~12% of votes in had the ANC ~32% narrowly ahead of the DA ~30% as of 0412 GMT 5 Nov, still unrepresentative (Reuters via Internazionale); IEC official results due Sunday 8 November (novanews). MTBPS temporary fuel-levy relief (diesel -R3/l, petrol -R1.50/l) in effect through 1 December.

full entry

Analysis. Wednesday 4 November - the triple-stacked event day finally lands, and the read going in is a quietly firmer tape rather than event-risk paralysis. Polls are open for the municipal elections, the MTBPS temporary fuel-levy relief (diesel -R3.00/l, petrol -R1.50/l) takes effect today, and OPEC+ has moved to pause its scheduled Q1 output hikes - a combination that, with Brent already easing toward ~$105 on US-Iran peace-talk hopes, pulls the near-term inflation threat down rather than up. Gold extended its bounce off the 30 October one-year low ($3,876) to $4,046/oz, back above $4,000 for the first time since the global AI-rout, and platinum firmed to $1,473; the rand held near R16.64 and the JSE All Share slipped a marginal 0.23% to 109,275 on the 3 November close, consolidating after the rout rather than extending it. The honest read is continuity with a constructive tilt: nothing today changes the standing hold-into-19-November call, and the softer oil path actively strengthens it.

Policy. The event day resolves two of the three live variables in a disinflationary direction, so the standing posture firms rather than shifts. For the SARB: the OPEC+ decision to pause its Q1 output hikes alongside Brent easing under ~$105 on peace-talk hopes removes the imported-fuel case for a pre-emptive hike, so the MPC should hold at 7.25% on 19 November and keep guidance data-dependent, letting the first post-levy October CPI (~25 Nov) arrive before judging any follow-through - the open Nedbank-hike-vs-hold split is itself the argument for patience. For Treasury: the fuel-levy relief taking effect today is the right cyclical cushion, but it is temporary (through 1 December) and the mining-windfall revenue assumption under the MTBPS still needs active defence with gold only just back above $4,000 and PGMs deeply oversold; protecting the consolidation path matters most into the 27 November Moody's and S&P reviews, where the fiscal-credibility dividend is the payoff. On the elections: whatever the metro outcomes, the macro task is the same - coalition stability in Johannesburg and Tshwane is now the binding constraint on service delivery and capex execution, not the rate path.

full entry

Analysis. Tuesday 3 November, the eve of a genuinely triple-stacked Wednesday - municipal elections, the MTBPS fuel-levy relief taking effect, and the OPEC+ output decision all land on the 4th - and the honest read is a quietly constructive consolidation with one correction to my own record. The rand firmed to R16.6418/$ (NEER up to 99.35) and the curve richened a touch (R2030 7.945%, R2036 8.67%), while gold eased to $3,933/oz and platinum held at $1,441, still deeply oversold; Brent recovered to ~$105.6/bbl after Monday's ~7.6% plunge, which today's wire sources attribute less to OPEC+ positioning than to US-Iran / Strait-of-Hormuz tension and US-midterm risk - a narrative shift from yesterday's entry worth flagging. The correction: yesterday I carried the JSE at a stale ~107,622 and called it flat, but TradingEconomics in fact has the All Share rebounding +1.77% to 109,527.80 on 2 November, so Monday was a solid risk-on recovery, not the flat tape I described. None of this disturbs the standing hold-at-7.25% call into the 19 November MPC - softer oil and tomorrow's fuel-levy relief keep tilting the inflation balance toward the hold camp against Nedbank's hike case - but tomorrow's municipal poll is the real near-term swing factor, with the ANC polling 22-32% nationally and the DA leading Johannesburg and Tshwane.

Policy. Tomorrow stacks three live decisions, and the standing posture firms rather than shifts. For the SARB: with CPI at 4.5%, the rand holding near R16.64 through the global rout, the long end still richening (R2030 7.945%), Brent back under $106 and the 4 November fuel-levy relief about to pull November CPI lower, the case for Nedbank's insurance hike is thinner than a week ago - the right call on 19 November remains to hold at 7.25% and look through imported PPI (6.18%), reserving a hike only if the rand breaks the 17.40 line or October CPI surprises hard above ~5.5%. For Treasury: keep the R10bn fuel-levy relief strictly temporary (expiring on schedule by early January 2027) and resist election-week pressure to extend it, protecting the clean primary-surplus, credit-positive message into the 27 November Moody's and S&P reviews - the more so while the PGM-and-bullion windfall that funded the +R17.1bn revenue upgrade is only partly repaired (gold $3,933, platinum still oversold at $1,441). Post-poll, with coalitions the likely norm in the big metros, the fiscal discipline to watch is that municipal coalition instability does not reopen bailout or transfer pressure on an already-stretched national balance sheet.

full entry

Analysis. Monday 2 November, markets reopened after Friday's imported AI-rout and the read is a stabilisation, not a continuation of the slide: gold bounced off its one-year low to $3,965.6/oz (+2.3% from Friday's $3,876), platinum firmed to $1,458 (still deeply oversold), the JSE All Share steadied roughly flat near 107,622, and the rand held firm at R16.6795/$ (NEER 99.12) with the curve barely moved (R2030 8.005%, R2036 8.71%). The one genuinely fresh move is in oil: Brent cracked about 6% lower to ~$106.2/bbl as traders positioned ahead of the 4 November OPEC+ meeting, where the alliance debates whether to keep lifting output ~400kbpd/month - an oversupply signal that, if it holds, eases exactly the fuel second-round-inflation channel Nedbank leaned on for its November hike call. So this week's set-up tilts further toward my standing hold view for the 19 November MPC rather than away from it, even as Wednesday stacks three events at once: the municipal elections, the MTBPS fuel-levy relief taking effect, and that OPEC+ decision. Policy. Today's ~6% Brent drop ahead of the 4 November OPEC+ meeting removes, rather than adds to, the inflation case for a pre-emptive hike, so the standing posture firms up. Policy. The shock is imported and equity-led, so the standing domestic posture holds - but the mining-windfall assumption under the MTBPS now needs active defence. The metals-and-rand relief paying for the improvement is exactly the reversible kind the SARB's October Monetary Policy Review warned against leaning on, and gold rolling off its high ($4,147.5 from above $4,188 last week) is an early reminder of that. For the MPC, Monday's oil turn lower on the Russia-sanctions waiver modestly eases the fuel cost-push risk, but the first clean read on the 7 October pump-price pass-through only arrives in the October CPI in late November, so the case is still to hold at 7.25% on 19 November and stay explicitly data-dependent rather than pre-commit on a pre-shock September print.

Analysis. Thursday, 24 September - Heritage Day, SA markets closed - and the honest headline is that the SARB wrong-footed me and the run of my own recent entries: after weeks in which I held a repo HOLD at 7.00% as the clear base case, the MPC instead HIKED 25bp to 7.25% (prime 10.75%) on 23 September in a unanimous vote, effective 25 September, explicitly to steer inflation toward the new 3% midpoint as the Iran-war oil shock intensified rather than unwound. The move lands against a soft domestic backdrop - Q2 GDP contracted 0.2% q/q (the first contraction after six quarters of growth) and August CPI ticked up only to 4.4% from 4.3% - so this reads as a pre-emptive, expectations-anchoring hike, not a reaction to realised inflation. I read the balance of risks correctly (oil was always the swing factor) but got the level call wrong; the lesson logged for this ledger is that with CPI already near the 3% target the SARB's reaction function has turned asymmetric and pre-emptive, willing to tighten into growth weakness to defend the endpoint. Base case now: this is a one-and-done for the current window, with the bar for a follow-up move set at the November MPC by the September CPI print and the rand.

Policy. Having hiked to 7.25% to anchor expectations to the 3% midpoint, the SARB should now HOLD through the November MPC and let the move transmit rather than compound it. The case for pausing is the growth side: Q2 GDP already contracted 0.2% q/q with mining and trade leading the fall, so a second consecutive hike into a shrinking economy risks over-tightening for an oil shock the bank cannot control with rates. The credibility-preserving stance is data-dependent patience - signal readiness to move again only if September/October CPI breaks convincingly above ~5% or the rand turns disorderly beyond ~17.10, and otherwise treat 7.25% as the terminal rate for this cycle while the fuel pass-through is monitored.

Net: the oil pullback is a modest relief, not a reversal - the imported-fuel upside for the ~24 September August CPI is intact, and the risk balance into the knife-edge 23 September MPC stays tilted hawkish.

Policy. Hold at 7.00% on 23 September and keep the guidance hawkish, but do not hike off an oil spike that is already fading at the margin. The standing structural point holds: a rules-based fuel-levy stabilisation band would let Treasury absorb oil-and-rand shocks at source rather than leaving the repo rate to arbitrate a supply spike it cannot fix.

Policy. The same decision that spared borrowers sent the rand down 2% toward R17, and a weaker currency imports exactly the second-round pressure the MPC says it wants to avoid, precisely as Brent breaks $99 on Red Sea and Caspian supply shocks. I am not re-running my hike call - the Committee saw the growth risk (1.4% is still a stall) and chose to protect it - but the episode sharpens the fiscal point this ledger has pressed since 3 July: a rules-based fuel-levy stabilisation band would let Treasury absorb an oil-and-rand shock at source, instead of leaving the repo rate to arbitrate between a supply spike it cannot fix and a growth path it dare not choke.

Analysis. Three days from the MPC and the most important new input landed over the weekend, and it cuts my way: the BER Inflation Expectations Survey shows average expectations across analysts, businesses and trade unions jumping from 3.6% to 4.4% for 2026, with 2027 at 4.2%, 2028 at 3.9% and the five-year at 4.1% (FNB weekly, reported by IOL/Business Report 19 Jul). That is the de-anchoring I asserted on 19 July when I wrote that expectations un-anchor cheaply and re-anchor expensively - now measured rather than argued, and the strongest evidence yet for the hike I called on 16 July. The same piece hands me a problem I should name rather than bury: the June CPI consensus has drifted DOWN toward 4.5%, not up to the 4.7% I have been quoting - Els (PSG) 4.7%, Hodes (Investec) 4.6%, Madikizela (Unisa) 4.4-4.6% with consensus at no change from May - so the live risk on my 4.5-5.0% band is now its floor, not its ceiling, and a 4.4% print scores that leg a miss. Policy. The SARB should hike 25bp to 7.25% on 23 July, and the BER survey is now the cleanest justification on the table: when average expectations move roughly 80bp in a single quarter to 4.4% for 2026 - above the 3% point target and level with the Bank's own revised CPI forecast - the MPC is no longer managing a fuel spike, it is defending the target itself. The counter-case is stronger today than it was yesterday and I should say so: July's already-announced R1.96/litre petrol cut mechanically pulls headline back toward Els's 4.3% next month, so the Bank would be hiking into an inflation rate that is about to turn down, which is precisely why Els argues for a hold. I still favour acting, because a hike delivered while expectations are visibly de-anchoring is cheaper than one delivered after wage settlements have priced 4.4% in. On fiscal, the rules-based fuel-levy stabilisation band this ledger has pressed since 3 July would have absorbed both June's +143c/litre and July's -196c/litre swings without the repo rate having to arbitrate either.

The signal that actually changed: Polymarket now prices a 52.5% chance of a 25bp HIKE on 23 July versus ~29% for no change, so the market has crossed from 'hold with hike optionality' (my 9 July framing) to a coin-flip-plus tilt toward tightening. I am not yet following it: my base case stays a hold, which makes this a clean, checkable disagreement with the market rather than a hedge.

Policy. With Brent easing back toward $76 as the Strait keeps flowing, the acute imported-inflation trigger has softened at the margin, and the SARB should hold at 7.00% on 23 July rather than validate the market's hike bet into a shock that is already partly reversing — lean on the July fuel cushion and base effects, but keep the statement explicitly conditional and armed to hike if Brent sustains a break above ~$90 or June/July CPI surprises above 5%. If the Bank does hike, it should frame it as one-off oil insurance, not the start of a cycle, to avoid over-tightening a 1.2%-growth economy. On the fiscal side the case is now overdue: two oil shocks in two months make a rules-based fuel-levy stabilisation band — flagged in this ledger since 3 July — the right structural fix so the next supply swing is absorbed fiscally rather than through the policy rate, alongside continued logistics and electricity reform to lift the growth floor.
